# From this point on

It’s very similar to *“going forward”*, but it feels a bit **stronger and more immediate**.

* **Going forward** → slightly formal, common in business/professional speech.

* **From this point on** → more direct, often used when marking a **clear change in approach** or **a decision moment**.

### **✅ Usage in spoken English:**

* *“From this point on, I’ll be more careful with my words.”*

* *“We’re not accepting late submissions from this point on.”*

* *“From this point on, things will be different.”*

### **📐 Examples in Architecture & Solution Design context:**

1. **System Architecture**

   * *“From this point on, all new modules will follow microservices principles.”*

   * *“From this point on, container orchestration will be handled by Kubernetes.”*

2. **Solution Design**

   * *“From this point on, we’ll use a unified design pattern to ensure consistency across applications.”*

   * *“From this point on, caching will be implemented to improve performance.”*

3. **Data & Integration**

   * *“From this point on, every data pipeline must include error-handling and logging.”*

   * *“From this point on, all API calls will be authenticated using OAuth2.”*

4. **Security**

   * *“From this point on, role-based access control will be strictly enforced.”*

   * *“From this point on, security patches must be applied within 48 hours.”*

5. **Project/Process**

   * *“From this point on, all requirements will go through a formal approval process.”*

   * *“From this point on, every sprint review will include architecture validation.”*

👉 Quick difference in tone:

* *“Going forward, we’ll follow microservices.”* → polite, future-oriented.

* *“From this point on, we’ll follow microservices.”* → firm, immediate decision.

# get along / get along with

verb

have a harmonious or friendly relationship

they seem to get along pretty well

he does not get along with his son

### **Meaning of "Get Along"**

"Get along" has two main meanings depending on the context:

### **1\. To have a good relationship with someone**

* **Meaning**: When two people are friendly and can work or live together without conflicts.  
* **Examples**:  
  * "I get along with my coworkers." (I have a good relationship with them.)  
  * "Do you and your siblings get along?" (Are you friendly with each other?)

### **2\. To manage or survive (often despite difficulties)**

* **Meaning**: To handle a situation or make progress, especially when things are not perfect.  
* **Examples**:  
  * "How are you getting along in your new job?" (How are you managing in the new job?)  
  * "We don’t have much money, but we’re getting along fine." (We are surviving or managing well.)

**Get along (with)** – To have a good relationship.  
 I get along well with my sister.

### **Usage Tips**

* Use **"get along with"** when talking about relationships:  
  * "I get along well with my classmates."  
* Use **"get along"** alone when talking about handling life or situations:

# Get Going

as in begin. to take the first step in (a process or course of action) She'd procrastinated long enough and it was time to get going. begin. Start.

phrase of go  
1\.  
leave a place in order to go somewhere else.  
"it's been wonderful seeing you again, but I think it's time we got going"  
2\.  
start happening or taking place.  
"the campaign got going in 1983"

The phrase **"Get going"** means to start moving, taking action, or beginning something. It is often used informally to encourage someone to start doing something or to leave a place. It conveys a sense of urgency or motivation.

### **Examples in daily use:**

1. **Encouraging action:**  
   * "If we want to catch the train, we need to get going now\!"  
     (Means you should start moving quickly to catch the train.)  
2. **Starting work or tasks:**  
   * "I have a lot of assignments to complete, so I should get going."  
     (Means you should start working on them.)  
3. **Motivating someone:**  
   * "Stop procrastinating and get going with your workout\!"  
     (Encourages someone to begin exercising.)  
4. **Leaving a place:**  
   * "It’s getting late. I think we should get going."  
     (Means you should start leaving.)  
5. **Initiating a process:**  
   * "Let’s get going on the project before the deadline sneaks up on us."  
     (Start working on the project.)

# Get into

### **1️⃣ To become involved in / start participating in something**

This is about starting to do or take part in an activity, hobby, job, or topic.

* **Meaning:** Begin engaging with or entering a field/activity.

* **Examples in spoken English:**

  * *"I’m thinking of getting into photography."*

  * *"She recently got into yoga and loves it."*

  * *"How did you get into cloud computing?"*

### **2️⃣ To develop an interest in something**

Often about starting to enjoy or follow something more deeply.

* **Meaning:** Start liking or becoming passionate about something.

* **Examples:**

  * *"I’m really getting into sci-fi movies these days."*

  * *"He’s getting into jazz music lately."*

### **3️⃣ To be accepted or admitted somewhere**

Usually for schools, colleges, clubs, organizations.

* **Meaning:** Gain entry or approval to join.

  * *"She got into Harvard\!"*

  * *"I’m trying to get into that members-only club."*

### **4️⃣ To discuss something in detail**

Used when you start explaining more deeply.

* **Meaning:** Delve deeper into a topic or issue.

  * *"Let’s not get into politics right now."*

  * *"Can you get into the details of the architecture design?"*

### **5️⃣ To be in the mood for something / to start feeling it**

This is more emotional or situational.

* **Meaning:** Become mentally/emotionally engaged.

  * *"The crowd is really getting into the concert."*

  * *"I couldn’t get into the movie—it was too slow."*

💡 **Key point:**

“Pull up” always involves either **movement toward/into position** or **bringing something into action/view** — whether it’s a car, a chair, a document, or even your own body in a workout.

Start being deeply involved.

# Gloss Over

The phrase **"gloss over"** (not "glass over") is a **phrasal verb** that means:

**To cover up, hide, or treat something (usually negative or unpleasant) as if it's not important.**

It’s often used when someone avoids going into detail about a mistake, problem, or sensitive topic.

### **🔹 How to Use "Gloss Over" in Spoken English:**

#### **1\. Talking about Avoiding Details**

* "He just glossed over the issue and moved on."

* "Don’t gloss over your responsibilities — be honest about what went wrong."

* "They glossed over the financial losses during the presentation."

#### **2\. In a Job or Interview Context**

* "You can’t gloss over your gaps in employment — be ready to explain them."

* "She tried to gloss over her lack of experience with fancy words."

#### **3\. In Personal Conversations**

* "You always gloss over your feelings. Why don’t you open up?"

* "He glossed over what happened last night. I could tell he was hiding something."

### **💡 Pro Tip:**

**Tone matters.** "Gloss over" often has a **negative or critical** tone. It implies someone is **not being transparent** or is **avoiding the truth**.

Skip important details.

# Go off / Went off

The phrase "go off" or "went off" has several meanings depending on the context. Here are the most common ones:

1\. To explode or make a loud noise  
Refers to something like a bomb, alarm, or firework exploding or sounding.  
Example:  
"The bomb went off in the middle of the night."  
"The fire alarm went off, and everyone had to evacuate."  
**2\. To stop working or turn off**  
Refers to lights, machines, or power shutting down.  
Example:  
"The lights went off during the storm."  
"The heater goes off automatically after an hour."  
3\. **To leave or depart**  
Refers to someone leaving a place or starting a journey.  
Example:  
"She went off to college last year."  
"He went off without saying goodbye."  
4\. To get angry or react emotionally  
Refers to someone suddenly getting upset or losing their temper.  
Example:  
"He went off on his coworker during the meeting."  
"Why did she go off like that? It wasn’t a big deal."  
5\. To decay or spoil (food)  
Refers to food becoming bad or unsuitable to eat.  
Example:  
"The milk has gone off; it smells terrible."  
"Don’t leave the fish out too long, or it’ll go off."  
6\. To stop liking something  
Refers to losing interest or enthusiasm for something.  
Example:  
"I’ve gone off sweet desserts recently."  
"He went off playing football after he injured his knee."

# Go Through

The phrase **"go through"** has multiple meanings depending on the context. Here are some common uses with memorable examples:

### **1️⃣ To Experience Something (Especially Difficulties) 😓**

* She had to **go through** a lot of struggles before achieving success.  
* I can’t imagine what you’re **going through** right now.

### **2️⃣ To Examine or Review Something 🔍**

* Before submitting your report, **go through** it carefully for errors.  
* Let’s **go through** the contract one more time before signing.

### **3️⃣ To Be Approved or Completed ✅**

* The deal didn’t **go through** because of legal issues.  
* Her visa application finally **went through** after months of waiting.

### **4️⃣ To Use Up Resources 🔥**

* We **went through** all our savings during the trip.  
* He **goes through** two cups of coffee every morning.

### **5️⃣ To Pass from One End to Another 🏃‍♂️**

* We **went through** a dark tunnel during the train ride.  
* The bullet **went through** the wall\!

**Meaning:** To experience or endure something.

🗣 *“She’s going through a tough time.”*  
 🗣 *“We went through all the files again.”*

To Read

I have gone through this book many a time.

To Face  
He had to go through such a trouble.

**Meaning:** To experience or endure something difficult or to examine something carefully.  
**Example:** She went through a tough time after the accident.  
**Context:** Emotional challenges, reviews, inspections.

Experience something (often difficult); also means to examine carefully.

# Go through the motions

The phrase **“go through the motions”** means to **do something in a mechanical or routine way, without real effort, energy, or enthusiasm**. It’s like you’re just doing the steps because you have to, not because you actually care.

### **🔹 Examples in sentences**

* *He’s been going through the motions at work ever since he stopped caring about the job.*

* *During the rehearsal, the actors just went through the motions—they weren’t truly performing.*

* *Sometimes in relationships, people stop trying and just go through the motions.*

👉 In **spoken English**, it’s often used when someone is **disengaged** or **acting without passion**:

* *I felt like I was just going through the motions at the gym today.*

* *She goes through the motions in class but never really participates.*

* *Don’t just go through the motions—put some heart into it\!*

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Going forward

“**Going forward**” means **from now on, in the future, or moving ahead**.  
 It’s often used in spoken English in professional contexts (meetings, presentations, discussions) to signal what will happen after the current moment.

It’s a polite and forward-looking phrase, often replacing “from now on” or “in the future.”

### **✅ Usage in spoken English:**

* *“Going forward, we’ll need to prioritize customer feedback more.”*

* *“This mistake won’t be repeated going forward.”*

* *“Going forward, let’s align our efforts with the new strategy.”*

### **📐 Examples from Architecture & Solution Design side:**

1. **System Architecture**

   * *“Going forward, the system will move from a monolithic design to a microservices-based architecture.”*

   * *“We should adopt containerization going forward to ensure better scalability.”*

2. **Solution Design**

   * *“Going forward, the solution will incorporate AI-driven analytics to improve decision-making.”*

   * *“We need to ensure high availability going forward by using load balancers.”*

3. **Data & Integration**

   * *“Going forward, all APIs should be designed using REST standards for consistency.”*

   * *“The data migration strategy will be automated going forward to reduce manual effort.”*

4. **Security**

   * *“Going forward, multi-factor authentication will be mandatory for all users.”*

   * *“Security audits will be part of the CI/CD pipeline going forward.”*

5. **Project/Process**

   * *“Going forward, we’ll follow an agile delivery model instead of waterfall.”*

   * *“Going forward, we’ll document all architectural decisions in a central repository.”*

idiom

refers to the future or the time ahead

From now on; in the future.

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Goof around

The phrase **“goof around”** is an **informal phrasal verb** that means to **waste time by being silly, playful, or not serious**. It’s often used when people are joking, laughing, or not focusing on what they’re supposed to be doing. 😅

### **🔹 Examples in sentences**

* *Stop goofing around and finish your homework.*

* *We were just goofing around at the park, playing games and laughing.*

* *The kids goofed around all afternoon instead of studying.*

👉 In **spoken English**, it’s a light, casual way to say “mess around” or “play around”:

* *Come on, don’t goof around—we’re late\!*

* *We were goofing around with the camera and took some funny pictures.*

* *He always goofs around during meetings, making everyone laugh.*

"I handed in my assignment late and lost a few points."
"He handed in his resignation the same week the reorg was announced."

"The project was handed off to a new team after the reorg."

# Hand out

 **Figurative meaning:** To give something freely — sometimes used critically when something is too easy to get.

🗣 *“The teacher handed out the exam sheets.”*  
 🗣 *“They don’t hand out promotions — you have to earn them.”*

* To distribute or give items to multiple people.  
* To pass around something.  
* Example 1: "The teacher handed out the test papers to all students."  
* Example 2: "Volunteers handed out food and water to those in need."

To distribute.

# Hands-off request

* **Figurative:** A polite way of saying “don’t get involved.”

* **Examples:**

  * “She gave me a *hands-off look* when I tried to help.”

# Hang on

**“Hang on”** is a very common phrase in spoken English. It has a few meanings depending on the situation.

### **1️⃣ Meaning: *Wait for a short time***

People use it when they want someone to pause briefly.

**Example:**  
 “Hang on, I’ll check the file and get back to you.”

👉 In meetings, phone calls, or conversations, this is the most common usage.

### **2️⃣ Meaning: *Hold something tightly***

Used in physical situations.

**Example:**  
 “Hang on to the railing while going downstairs.”

### **3️⃣ Meaning: *Stay strong / don’t give up***

Used emotionally or motivationally.

**Example:**  
 “Hang on, things will improve soon.”

### **Natural spoken sentences you can practice**

Try saying these aloud:

* “Hang on, let me confirm that and get back to you.”  
* “Just hang on a second—I’m opening the document.”  
* “Hang on, I think there’s another approach we can try.”  
* “Hang on, before we finalize this, let’s review the assumptions.”

These are very useful in **client calls, architecture discussions, and technical meetings**.

### **Useful connectors to make your speech smoother**

You can combine *hang on* with linking phrases:

* “Hang on, **before that**, let me clarify one point.”  
* “Hang on, **just to be sure**, are we talking about the production environment?”  
* “Hang on, **that being said**, we may need another validation step.”

### **Other contexts where “hang on” is commonly used**

**Phone conversation**  
 “Hang on, I’ll transfer your call.”

**Debugging discussion**  
 “Hang on, I think the issue is coming from the API layer.”

**Presentation**  
 “Hang on, let me explain this architecture flow step by step.”

If you’d like, I can also explain the difference between **“hang on,” “hold on,” and “wait a second”**—they sound similar but are used slightly differently in professional conversations.

# have had

Let me explain the use of "have had" and provide some memorable examples.

"Have had" is used in the present perfect tense to describe:  
1\. Past experiences that are still relevant now  
2\. Actions that started in the past and continue into the present  
3\. Recently completed actions with present consequences

Here are some memorable examples:

"I have had this watch since my graduation."  
(Shows ownership starting in the past and continuing to now)

"She has had three cups of coffee today."  
(Describes completed actions within a time period that hasn't ended)

"They have had many adventures together over the years."  
(Talks about accumulated experiences up to the present)

"We have had our share of disagreements, but we're still best friends."  
(Describes past experiences affecting the present relationship)

"The doctor has had to cancel all appointments due to an emergency."  
(Indicates a recent action with current impact)

Would you like me to explain any of these examples in more detail?

"I have had this watch since my graduation."

# have it [done]

The phrase **“have it \[done\]”** is an example of a **causative construction** in English.

* In this structure, the subject doesn’t do the action directly but **arranges for someone else** to do it.

* The form is usually:  
   **have \+ object \+ past participle**

🔹 Examples:

* *I’ll have the report prepared by tomorrow.* → (I won’t prepare it myself; I’ll arrange for someone else to do it.)

* *She had her car washed yesterday.* → (She didn’t wash it; someone else did.)

* *Please have this picked up.* → (You’re asking someone to arrange for the pickup, not to do it themselves necessarily.)

So in your sentence **“Please have this picked up on priority”**,

* **“have”** \= causative verb

* **“this”** \= object

* **“picked up”** \= past participle

### **1\. Have it done (formal, neutral, businesslike)**

* Structure: **have \+ object \+ past participle**

* Meaning: arrange for someone else to do it.

* Tone: more **professional, polite, or formal**.

* Example:

  * *I’ll have the documents signed by tomorrow.*

  * (I’ll make sure someone signs them, not me personally.)

### **2\. Get it done (casual, direct, sometimes urgent)**

* Structure: **get \+ object \+ past participle**

* Meaning: ensure the task is completed, often more urgent or informal.

* Tone: more **informal, commanding, or motivational**.

  * *I need to get this report finished before the deadline.*

  * (Either I’ll do it myself or push to make sure it’s done quickly.)

🔹 **Key difference in feeling:**

* **“Have it done”** → neutral, formal, professional (good for emails, Jira, business).

* **“Get it done”** → informal, urgent, sometimes motivational (good in casual speech, team pep talks).

👉 So in your Jira context, **“Please have this picked up as a priority”** is better than **“Please get this picked up”**, which could sound a little too blunt.

# Head down/Heads down

### **1\. Literal / Physical meaning**

* **Meaning:** Your head is lowered, often because you’re focusing on something, feeling tired, shy, or trying to avoid attention.

* **Example:**

  * *She walked past me with her head down, not saying a word.*

  * *He kept his head down to avoid the cold wind.*

### **2\. Idiomatic meaning — “focus and work hard”**

* **Meaning:** To concentrate completely on a task, usually without distractions, until it’s finished.

  * *I have a big deadline tomorrow, so I’ll keep my head down and finish this project.*

  * *We all had to put our heads down and study for the exam.*

### **3\. Idiomatic meaning — “stay out of trouble”**

* **Meaning:** To avoid attracting attention, especially to avoid problems or criticism.

  * *There’s a lot of office politics right now — better keep your head down.*

  * *He kept his head down after the argument so he wouldn’t get dragged into it.*

### **1\. Head down (singular)**

* Used when referring to **your own** head or a single person’s head.

* Often in **idioms** like:

  * *keep your head down* → stay out of trouble or focus on work.

  * *head down to \[place\]* → move towards a place (different meaning).

* **Examples:**

  * *I’m going to keep my head down and finish this report.*

  * *She walked past me with her head down.*

### **2\. Heads down (plural)**

* Used when talking about **multiple people** all lowering their heads (literally or figuratively).

* Also used in **group-focused idioms**:

  * *Heads down, everyone\!* → telling a group to focus or avoid attention.

  * *During the storm, we all kept our heads down and waited it out.*

  * *The coach said, “Heads down, team — we still have work to do\!”*

✅ **Rule of thumb for spoken English:**

* Talking about **yourself** or **one person** → *head down*.

* Talking to/about **a group** → *heads down*.

If you want to say you’ll work with focus to solve a bug, you could naturally say:

* **“I’m going to keep my head down and fix this bug.”**

* **“I’ll have my head down working on this bug until it’s resolved.”**

* **“I’ll be heads down debugging for the rest of the day.”** ← here *“heads down”* is also common in tech/work culture to mean *deeply focused*, even for one person.

💡 In modern workplace English, especially in tech, people often use **“heads down”** informally to mean *I’ll be deeply focused*, even though technically it’s plural. So you might hear:

*“I’ll be heads down on this until EOD.”*  
 *“I was heads down debugging all morning.”*

So:

* **Grammatically strict** → “keep my head down” (singular) for yourself.

* **Workplace slang** → “be heads down” is fine and common.

# Hear me out

**“Please listen to everything I have to say before reacting or judging.”**

It’s used when you're trying to explain something important, defend yourself, or share an idea — especially if you expect resistance or misunderstanding.

**🔹 How to Use It in Sentences:**

**1\. During a disagreement:**

* "I know it sounds crazy, but hear me out — this might actually work."

* "Before you say no, just hear me out."

**2\. When offering an idea or opinion:**

* "Hear me out: What if we shifted the launch to next month?"

* "I’ve got a plan. Hear me out before you decide."

**3\. When explaining a mistake:**

* "I messed up, yes. But hear me out — there’s more to it."

**💡 Tone Tip:**

Saying "hear me out" politely, with a calm voice and eye contact, makes people more likely to **actually** listen.

# Herculean task

A **“Herculean task”** means a job or duty that is **extremely difficult, demanding, or requires a lot of strength and effort**.

The phrase comes from **Hercules**, a hero in Greek mythology known for his superhuman strength and for completing the *Twelve Labors*, which were nearly impossible challenges. So, when you say something is a *Herculean task*, you’re comparing it to those legendary feats.

✅ **Examples in sentences (spoken style):**

* *“Migrating all our data to the new system was a Herculean task, but we managed it.”*

* *“Convincing him to change his mind feels like a Herculean task.”*

* *“Finishing the project in one week would be a Herculean task for any team.”*

✨ **Useful spoken phrases for meetings or casual talk:**

1. *“That’s going to be a Herculean task, but I think we can pull it off.”*

2. *“On the other hand, if we split the work, the Herculean task becomes manageable.”*

3. *“It looked like a Herculean effort at first, but once we started, things fell into place.”*

4. *“Honestly, handling this with such a small team feels like a Herculean challenge.”*

# hit up

**“Hit up”** is an informal phrasal verb. It usually means:

1. **To contact someone** (call, text, message)  
2. **To ask someone for something**  
3. Sometimes, **to go to a place**

### **1️⃣ Hit up \= Contact someone**

This is the most common meaning in spoken English.

**Example:**

* “I’ll hit you up later.”  
   👉 I’ll contact you later.  
* “Hit me up when you reach home.”  
   👉 Contact me when you reach home.

This is very casual and mostly used with friends or colleagues (not very formal settings).

### **2️⃣ Hit up \= Ask for something**

* “He hit me up for some money.”  
   👉 He asked me for money.  
* “She hit her manager up for a promotion.”  
   👉 She asked her manager for a promotion.

Here, it slightly implies requesting or seeking help.

### **3️⃣ Hit up \= Go to a place**

* “Let’s hit up that new café.”  
   👉 Let’s go to that new café.

This is also casual.

## **In professional/meeting context (be careful)**

In client calls or formal meetings (which you often attend as an Architect), avoid using “hit up.”

Instead say:

* “I’ll reach out to you.”  
* “I’ll get in touch.”  
* “Let me connect with you offline.”

Because “hit up” sounds too casual for enterprise conversations.

## **Natural Spoken Sentences You Can Practice**

Try speaking these aloud:

1. “I’ll hit you up once I finish this deployment.”  
2. “If you need anything, just hit me up.”  
3. “Let’s hit up the DevOps team and clarify the dependency.”  
4. “I was planning to hit him up regarding the architecture changes.”  
5. “That being said, I’ll hit you up tomorrow with the updated numbers.”

Notice how I used **“That being said”** to improve flow. 👌

## **Extra Context of Usage**

* Very common in **American English**  
* Mostly used in **texting, Slack, WhatsApp**  
* Casual tone  
* Not recommended in formal emails

ask someone for something, typically money

I have an employee who is always hitting me up for a raise

he hit up some family members

if you have a serious band and would like to play some music, hit me up

(Informal) To contact someone; also to ask for something.

# Hold back

**“Hold back”** means **to stop yourself from doing something**, **delay something**, or **restrain emotions, actions, or progress** depending on the context.

Common meanings with examples

1. **Control emotions**  
* *He was trying to hold back his tears.*  
   👉 He was stopping himself from crying.  
2. **Delay progress**  
* *This issue is holding back the deployment.*  
   👉 The issue is slowing things down.  
3. **Not share information**  
* *Please don’t hold back any details.*  
   👉 Tell everything openly.  
4. **Reduce speed or movement**  
* *The traffic held us back.*  
   👉 Traffic delayed us.

In technical or workplace discussions (especially useful for architecture or MLOps conversations), it often means:

* blocking progress  
* slowing execution  
* preventing rollout  
* delaying decisions

For example:

The dependency on another team is holding us back from releasing this feature.

Natural spoken sentences you can use

Try saying these aloud:

* *To be honest, I didn’t want to hold back my opinion during the meeting.*  
* *Right now, the infrastructure limitation is holding us back.*  
* *If there’s any concern, please don’t hold back—let’s discuss it openly.*  
* *We were ready to deploy; however, one blocker held us back.*  
* *That being said, we shouldn’t hold back progress because of minor issues.*

Useful connectors to improve flow

You can combine **hold back** with linking phrases like:

* *For example,* this dependency is holding us back.  
* *On the other hand,* removing this step could speed things up.  
* *That being said,* we shouldn’t hold back the release too long.  
* *At the same time,* we don’t want to rush without validation.

For example, the missing API contract is holding us back; however, once it’s finalized, we can move quickly.

Other contexts where “hold back” is useful

You can use it in:

* meetings → *This requirement is holding us back.*  
* presentations → *Legacy systems are holding back innovation.*  
* parenting → *Don’t hold back your questions.*  
* self-development → *Fear sometimes holds us back from trying new things.*

Delay or restrain.

# Hold on

**Hold on** – To wait.  
 Hold on a second, I'll be right back.

“Hold on” is another phrase with a few common uses:

1. **Wait a moment** (most common in conversation)

   * *“Hold on, I’ll check and get back to you.”*

2. **Stay strong / don’t give up**

   * *“I know times are tough, but hold on—it will get better.”*

3. **Physically grip something**

   * *“Hold on to the railing when you go down the stairs.”*

👉 In everyday speech, when someone says *“Hold on”*, they usually mean *“Wait a second.”*

Meaning: To wait or remain in a position.

* Example 1: Please hold on while I transfer your call to the appropriate department.  
* Example 2: Hold on a second—I’ll grab my notebook and join you.

To wait; also means to grip tightly.

# Hold up

Meaning: To delay or cause a delay.

* Example 1: The shipment was held up due to customs clearance issues.  
* Example 2: Sorry for being late—the traffic held me up.

**✅ 1\. Delayed / made late (most common)**

When something is held up, it means it was delayed unexpectedly.

Examples

Sorry I’m late — traffic held me up.

The meeting was held up because the manager arrived late.

My train got held up due to heavy rain.

👉 Think: held up \= slowed down / delayed

**✅ 2\. Stopped (often by police, security, or obstacles)**

We were held up at the checkpoint.

His car was held up by road construction.

**✅ 3\. Robbed (informal, usually “held up at gunpoint”)**

This is more dramatic and less common in daily conversation.

The shop was held up last night.

**✅ 4\. To survive or remain strong (“hold up well”)**

Used when someone or something is coping okay.

How are you holding up?

She’s holding up well after the surgery.

**Quick summary (for spoken English)**

Traffic held me up → I got delayed

We were held up at security → stopped/delayed

The store was held up → robbed

I’m holding up → I’m managing / I’m okay

**Literal meaning:** To lift or support something.  
 **Figurative meanings:**

* To delay

* To stay strong under pressure

* To use as an example

🗣 *“Traffic held us up for an hour.”*  
 🗣 *“How are you holding up after the exam?”*  
 🗣 *“That theory doesn’t hold up under scrutiny.”*

To delay; also means to rob someone (armed); or to remain strong.

# Hold/Held Up

phrasal verb of hold  
1\.  
support and prevent something from falling.  
"concrete pillars hold up the elevated section of the railway"

2\.  
display something by holding it above one's waist or head.  
"he held up the book so she could see the cover"

## 1\. "Hold up" (Present tense)

It is used in the present tense.  
Meanings:  
**Delay or stop:** "The traffic is holding us up."  
**Support or lift:** "These pillars hold up the building."  
**As a question (informal):** "Hold up\! What did you just say?" (meaning: "Wait a moment.")  
**To endure or remain strong:** "I hope she holds up under the pressure."

## 2\. "Held up" (Past tense)

It is the past tense or past participle of "hold up."  
Meanings:  
Was delayed or stopped: "The train was held up due to technical issues."  
Was supported or sustained: "The roof was held up by steel beams."  
Was robbed (as in a crime): "The bank was held up by two masked men."

# Hook on

### **🔹 1\. To attach / fasten something (literal)**

* *He hooked the trailer on to the truck.*

* *She hooked on her necklace before leaving the house.*

### **🔹 2\. To become very interested or addicted to something (informal, more common in spoken English)**

* *I got hooked on that new Netflix series—it’s so good\!*

* *He’s really hooked on video games lately.*

* *Once you try this dessert, you’ll be hooked on it.*

👉 Here, people often just say **“hooked on”** instead of “hook on.”

### **🔹 3\. To rely on / depend on (less common, figurative)**

* *The company is hooked on foreign investment.*

✅ **Spoken-style examples:**

* *I’m totally hooked on coffee—I can’t start my day without it.*

* *She got hooked on yoga after just one class.*

* *Don’t get hooked on junk food—it’s hard to quit.*

;

### **1\. Sacred**

Something very special, respected, or holy.  
 👉 *“Family time is sacred for me.”*

### **2\. I’m on it**

Means you’re already taking care of something.  
 👉 *“Don’t worry about the report, I’m on it.”*

### **3\. Savannah**

A flat grassland, often with few trees (like in Africa).  
 👉 *“Lions usually live in the savannah.”*

### **4\. Wreak havoc**

Cause chaos or serious damage.  
 👉 *“The storm wreaked havoc across the city.”*

### **5\. Vulnerable**

Easily hurt, affected, or open to danger.  
 👉 *“She felt vulnerable after losing her job.”*

### **6\. Set apart**

To make something different or special from others.  
 👉 *“Her creativity really sets her apart from others.”*

### **7\. Castoff**

Something or someone rejected or thrown away.  
 👉 *“He bought a castoff jacket from a thrift shop.”*

### **8\. Swear by**

To strongly trust or believe in something.  
 👉 *“I swear by green tea for good health.”*

### **9\. Mess up**

Make a mistake or ruin something.  
 👉 *“I totally messed up the presentation.”*

### **10\. Lead with**

Start or approach something with a particular focus.  
 👉 *“In interviews, I always lead with my strongest skill.”*

# Hook Up

verb  
(transitive) to assemble the parts of a mechanism, especially by connecting wires.  
(transitive) to connect to a power supply or a signal source.  
(intransitive, slang) to form an association (with) someone.

### **🔌 1\. To connect something (literally)**

Used when connecting devices or equipment.

**Examples:**

* “Can you help me hook up the TV to the sound system?”

* “I just hooked up my phone to the car’s Bluetooth.”

### **👯 2\. To meet or get together (casual social sense)**

This is informal and friendly, often about meeting people.

* “Let’s hook up for coffee sometime.”

* “We hooked up after the conference and talked for hours.”

### **❤️‍🔥 3\. To have a casual romantic or sexual encounter**

This is very informal and often implies physical intimacy. Be careful with this meaning—only use it when appropriate or with people who understand you're using it in this context.

* “They hooked up at the party last night.” (implies a romantic/sexual encounter)

### **💼 4\. To help someone get something / to arrange a deal**

Often used when you help someone get connected with an opportunity, product, or service.

* “He hooked me up with a job at his company.”

* “Can you hook me up with some concert tickets?”

### **🔁 Casual sentence practice:**

Try these out loud to get the feel:

* “I’ll hook you up with my trainer—he’s awesome.”

* “We hooked up with some old friends from college last weekend.”

* “I need to hook up my laptop to the projector.”

To connect (devices); slang for starting a romantic/sexual relationship.

Connect two systems.

# I couldn’t care less

**“I couldn’t care less”** is an informal phrase that means:  
 👉 *“I don’t care at all”* or *“It doesn’t matter to me.”*

It shows **zero interest or concern** about something.

### **Examples**

* *“He said I was late, but honestly, I couldn’t care less.”*

* *“Some people don’t like my style — I couldn’t care less.”*

* *“She couldn’t care less about the gossip.”*

### **Sentences You Can Use**

1. “They argued about it, but I couldn’t care less.”

2. “If he’s upset, I couldn’t care less.”

3. “People said it was risky, but I couldn’t care less.”

4. “Honestly, I couldn’t care less what they think.”

⚠️ **Tone warning**:

* This phrase can sound **rude or dismissive** if said sharply.

* In professional settings, it’s better to soften it:

  * “It doesn’t really concern me.”

  * “I don’t mind either way.”

  * “I’m neutral about that.”

✨ Practice aloud: *“Honestly, I couldn’t care less about that detail.”*

# I haven't thought it through

"I haven't thought it through" means you haven't fully considered all the possible outcomes or consequences of a situation or decision, and you need more time to think about it.   
Here's a more detailed explanation:  
Meaning:  
When you say "I haven't thought it through," you're essentially saying that you haven't given a situation or decision enough thought or consideration, and you haven't fully evaluated the potential results.   
Implication:  
It suggests a lack of thoroughness in your thinking process and a need for more time to analyze the situation before making a decision or taking action.   
Examples:  
"I'm not sure about that idea, I haven't thought it through yet."  
"I'm going to need some time to think it through before I make a decision."  
"I didn't think through the consequences of my actions."

# I want off this case

The phrase "I want off this case" usually means that someone wants to stop being involved in a particular situation, project, or task. It’s commonly used in contexts like work, investigations, or responsibilities. Here’s a breakdown:

Work or Assignment Context

It can mean you no longer want to work on a specific project or task.  
Example: "This case is too stressful, and I want off this case."  
Emotional Detachment

It could reflect frustration, burnout, or feeling overwhelmed, leading to a desire to step away.  
Example: "I can’t handle this anymore—I want off this case."  
Delegating Responsibility

You might want someone else to take over the situation.  
Example: "This isn’t my area of expertise. I want off this case."

# In Hindsight

“In hindsight” means looking back at a past situation and understanding it more clearly now than you did at the time.

🗣️ How to use “in hindsight” in spoken English:

Here are some examples you can use in conversation:

1. **General reflection:**

   * *"In hindsight, I should have prepared more for that interview."*

   * *(= Now that I think back, I realize I didn’t prepare enough.)*

2. **Learning from mistakes:**

   * *"It seemed like a good idea at the time, but in hindsight, it was a mistake."*

   * *(= I now realize it wasn’t a good idea.)*

3. **With a tone of regret or realization:**

   * *"In hindsight, quitting my job without a backup plan was risky."*

4. **When talking about decisions or strategies:**

   * *"In hindsight, we should have gone with the other vendor."*

### **🧠 Memory Tip:**

* Think of “hind” \= behind \= looking **backward in time**.

* It’s often used when people reflect on **what they should or could have done differently**.

# in the distance

The phrase **“in the distance”** means something that is far away but still visible or noticeable. It’s often used when you can see, hear, or sense something faintly because it’s not close to you.

For example:

* *“I saw mountains in the distance.”* (The mountains are far away, not nearby.)

* *“We could hear music playing in the distance.”* (The sound is faint because it’s far.)

It’s a descriptive phrase, often used in storytelling or daily conversation to give a sense of space or perspective.

### **Common sentences you could use in real life:**

1. *“I could see the lights of the city in the distance.”*

2. *“There was a storm forming in the distance, so we decided to head back.”*

3. *“On the other hand, if you look in the distance, you’ll notice the mountains.”*

4. *“In the distance, I noticed someone waving at me.”*

👉 Try practicing by describing what you can see or imagine: *“Right now, I can see trees in the distance from my window.”*

# It is imperative

The phrase **“it is imperative”** means **“it is absolutely necessary”** or **“it is very important and must be done.”**  
 It’s often used in formal or professional settings when stressing urgency or importance.

### **Examples:**

1. *It is imperative that we finish the project before the deadline.*  
    → (We **must** finish it, no excuses.)

2. *It is imperative to follow safety protocols in the lab.*  
    → (Following safety rules is **essential**.)

3. *It is imperative for leaders to listen to their teams.*  
    → (Leaders **must** listen, it’s not optional.)

### **Phrases you can use in conversation:**

* “It is imperative that we take immediate action.”

* “From my perspective, it is imperative to address this issue first.”

* “That being said, it is imperative to stay aligned with our goals.”

* “For example, in situations like this, it is imperative to communicate clearly.”

# It's a half measure

The phrase "it's a half measure" refers to an action or solution that is incomplete, insufficient, or not fully committed. It implies that the effort or approach taken is not enough to fully address the situation or achieve the desired result.

Examples:  
General example:

"They promised to fix the issue, but their response was just a half measure that didn’t solve the problem."  
Programmer's life:

"Adding basic error messages without implementing proper error handling is just a half measure. We need to ensure the system can handle unexpected inputs gracefully."  
It often carries a negative connotation, suggesting that a more thorough or decisive effort is needed.

 Used for acting silly, joking, or not focusing on work.

 When used about **yourself or friends**, it can be casual and playful.

# Jigsaw puzzle

A **jigsaw puzzle** is a game or picture that’s been cut into many irregularly shaped pieces which you fit together to complete the full image.

**Key points:**

* The name comes from the old method of cutting the pieces with a **jig saw** (a type of saw).

* Pieces are usually made of cardboard or wood.

* It can be a fun hobby, a way to relax, or even used as a metaphor.

**Figurative meaning:**

* Sometimes “jigsaw puzzle” is used to describe something complex made up of many interrelated parts.

  * *Understanding this case is like putting together a jigsaw puzzle.*

# Lagging behind

**Lagging behind**” means **falling behind others in progress, speed, or performance**. It’s often used when comparing progress to a standard, a group, or a timeline.

* In work: *“Our project is lagging behind schedule.”*

* In studies: *“He’s lagging behind his classmates in math.”*

* In general life: *“Some countries are lagging behind in adopting new technology.”*

It has a slightly **negative connotation**, implying that someone or something is not keeping up as expected.

### **Phrases you can use in conversations:**

1. “We seem to be lagging behind the deadline, so we need to speed things up.”

2. “On the other hand, if we don’t fix this, we might lag behind our competitors.”

3. “For example, our team is doing well in design, but we’re lagging behind in testing.”

4. “That being said, lagging behind now doesn’t mean we can’t catch up later.”

👉 Try practicing aloud with both professional and casual contexts, like talking about projects at work or even fitness goals with friends.

Would you like me to also show you some **alternative phrases** you could use instead of “lagging behind,” so your speech doesn’t sound repetitive?

##### **You said:**

what would i say if Im way behind my aspiration to become something

### **Natural ways to say it:**

* **“I’m way behind where I thought I’d be.”**

* **“I haven’t reached the point I had hoped for yet.”**

* **“I feel like I’m falling short of my aspirations right now.”**

* **“I’m not where I want to be yet, but I’m working on it.”**

* **“So far, I’m behind my own goals, but I’m still moving forward.”**

### **Example sentences for speaking practice:**

1. “Honestly, I’m way behind where I wanted to be in my career, but I’m trying to catch up.”

2. “On the other hand, being behind doesn’t mean I can’t get there eventually.”

3. “For example, I planned to achieve this in three years, but I’m still not there yet.”

4. “That being said, I’m learning along the way, so the journey still matters.”

✨ Notice that I softened the tone a little — this way, it doesn’t sound overly harsh or discouraging. It shows self-awareness **and** determination.

British  
spend money extravagantly.  
"I decided to lash out and treat myself"

to suddenly attack someone or something physically or criticize him, her, or it in an angry way: lash out (at) I was only teasing him and suddenly he lashed out (at me) and hit me in the face.

**“Lash out”** means to **suddenly express anger or frustration**, either **verbally or physically**. It’s often used when someone reacts harshly — shouting, criticizing, or even hitting — because they’re upset or under stress.

### **🔹 Common Meanings:**

1. **To attack verbally:**  
    → “He lashed out at his colleague for making a mistake.”  
    *(= He angrily criticized his colleague.)*

2. **To attack physically:**  
    → “The child lashed out when his toy was taken away.”  
    *(= He hit or kicked in anger.)*

3. **To express anger suddenly or without control:**  
    → “She’s been under a lot of pressure lately, so she sometimes lashes out at people.”

### **🔹 Spoken English Examples:**

* “Sorry for lashing out earlier — I was just really stressed.”

* “He tends to lash out when things don’t go his way.”

* “I didn’t mean to lash out, it just came out of frustration.”

* “When people feel cornered, they often lash out to protect themselves.”

* “Instead of lashing out, it’s better to take a deep breath and talk calmly.”

### **🔹 Connecting Phrases You Can Use:**

* “That being said, I shouldn’t have lashed out.”

* “For example, when someone criticizes my work, I sometimes lash out without realizing it.”

* “On the other hand, some people stay silent instead of lashing out.”

💬 **Practice tip:**  
 Try saying:

“I sometimes lash out when I’m overwhelmed, but I’m learning to handle stress better.”

# Lay behind

### **1\. Literal meaning**

* To be physically positioned at the back or rear.

  * *The garden lay behind the house.*

  * *A small park lay behind the school building.*

Here it simply means *was located at the back of*.

### **2\. Figurative meaning**

* To be the cause or reason for something (what “lay behind” an event \= what caused it).

  * *Jealousy lay behind his actions.* → The real reason for his actions was jealousy.

  * *Fear of failure lay behind her hesitation.*

  * *What lay behind the sudden policy change?*

So figuratively, it’s about the underlying **cause or hidden reason**.

### **In spoken style**

* *“What do you think lay behind his sudden anger?”* → What really caused it?

* *“I believe her insecurity lay behind those remarks.”*

✅ **Key idea:**

* **Literal:** at the back.

* **Figurative:** the deeper reason or hidden factor.

# Lay off

Meaning: To dismiss or terminate employees due to economic reasons.

* Example 1: Unfortunately, the company had to lay off several employees during the recession.  
* Example 2: The firm laid off 10% of its workforce to cut costs.

### **✅ Meaning of "Lay off":**

**"Lay off"** is a **phrasal verb** with different meanings based on context, but the most common are:

### **1\. Business / Employment Context**

➡️ **To lay someone off** means to **terminate their job**, usually due to **company downsizing, budget cuts, or restructuring**, **not because of poor performance**.

#### **🔹 Example sentences:**

* *The company had to lay off 200 workers during the recession.*

* *She was laid off last month due to cost-cutting measures.*

* *Being laid off is hard, especially when you’ve worked there for years.*

### **2\. Casual / Informal Context**

➡️ **"Lay off (something/someone)"** also means to **stop doing something**, especially when it's annoying, harmful, or excessive.

* *Hey, lay off the junk food — it’s not good for you.*

* *Can you lay off him? He’s already stressed out.*

* *You need to lay off the complaints and try to help instead.*

### **✅ Summary of Key Uses:**

| Phrase | Meaning | Example |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| **lay someone off** | Terminate a job (economic reason) | *He got laid off due to company losses.* |
| **lay off something** | Stop doing/using something (informal) | *You should lay off caffeine for a while.* |
| **lay off someone** | Stop bothering or criticizing someone | *Lay off her — she’s doing her best.* |

* To terminate employment; dismiss workers.  
* To stop bothering or criticizing someone.  
* Example 1: "The company had to lay off 100 employees due to budget cuts."  
* Example 2: "Lay off him\! He’s already doing his best."

To terminate from a job; also to stop doing something.

Repeated: To dismiss from a job.

# Lay Out / Laid Out

**"Lay out"** (verb) means to **arrange, organize, or present something in a clear and structured way**. The past tense of this phrase is **"laid out."**

### **Different Meanings & Usages:**

1. **To arrange or spread things in an orderly way**  
   * Example: He laid out his tools before starting the repair.  
   * Meaning: He arranged the tools properly before beginning work.  
2. **To explain something clearly and in detail**  
   * Example: The manager laid out the new strategy in the meeting.  
   * Meaning: The manager explained the new strategy step by step.  
3. **To design or structure something**  
   * Example: The UI of the app is well laid out for easy navigation.  
   * Meaning: The app's user interface is designed in a structured and user-friendly way.  
4. **To prepare something for a specific purpose**  
   * Example: I laid out my clothes for tomorrow’s interview.  
   * Meaning: I selected and arranged my clothes in advance.  
5. **To spend a large amount of money** (less common in IT)  
   * Example: He laid out a fortune on the latest tech gadgets.  
   * Meaning: He spent a lot of money on new tech devices.

### **General Daily Use:**

1. The instructions were clearly **laid out** on the packaging.  
2. He **laid out** his plans for the weekend in great detail.  
3. The rules were **laid out** before the competition started.  
4. I have **laid out** my clothes for tomorrow's meeting.  
5. She **laid out** all the ingredients before starting to cook.

### **IT & Work Context:**

1. The project roadmap was **laid out** in the presentation.  
2. The system architecture is **laid out** in the design document.  
3. The steps to deploy the application are clearly **laid out** in the manual.  
4. The database schema was **laid out** using a proper relational model.  
5. The company's security policies are **laid out** in the compliance guide.

# Lean into

The phrase **“lean into”** means to **embrace something fully, accept it, and move forward with confidence instead of resisting or avoiding it**.

It’s often used in professional and personal growth contexts.

### **Examples:**

* *“She decided to lean into the challenge instead of shying away from it.”* → She accepted the challenge and gave it her best effort.

* *“If you want to grow as a leader, you have to lean into uncomfortable conversations.”* → You need to accept and handle difficult conversations instead of avoiding them.

* *“He leaned into his creativity and started his own design company.”* → He embraced his talent and used it to move forward.

### **Phrases you can practice in conversations:**

1. *“I think we should lean into this opportunity rather than hold back.”*

2. *“Instead of avoiding the problem, let’s lean into it and find a solution.”*

3. *“She leaned into her strengths and that’s why the project succeeded.”*

4. *“On the other hand, if we don’t lean into this change, we might fall behind.”*

👉 Try saying these out loud with a natural flow.

"Leaped up" means to move quickly or suddenly, usually from one place to another. For example, "She leapt up to answer the phone".

"When the alarm went off, she leapt out of bed".  
"The crowd leapt to its feet".  
"I leapt up to answer the phone".  
"She leaped up to help him".

"She can leap up and touch the ceiling".  
"He would leap up at the sound of his name".  
Synonyms for "leaped up":   
bound, bounce, hop, rise, rocket, skip, soar, surge, and vault.

to make a large jump or sudden movement, usually from one place to another: He leaped out of his car and ran towards the house. I leaped up to answer the phone. The dog leaped over the gate into the field.

Jumped quickly; also used for sudden rise (prices, emotions).

# Leave it off

It can mean a few different things depending on the context:

1. **Stop doing something / Pause it**

   * Example: *“Let’s leave it off here and continue tomorrow.”*  
      (means stop for now).

2. **Keep something turned off**

   * Example: *“Should I turn on the fan?” → “No, leave it off.”*  
      (means don’t switch it on).

3. **Omit something**

   * Example: *“Do you want to include that detail in the report?” → “No, leave it off.”*  
      (means exclude it).

Here are some **spoken phrases** you can practice:

* *“Let’s leave it off for now and pick it up later.”*

* *“You can leave the lights off; natural light is enough.”*

* *“We can leave that part off the presentation to save time.”*

* *“If you don’t need it, just leave it off.”*

# Left off

**“Left off”** means **to stop something at a particular point**, usually with the intention of continuing later. It is commonly used in conversations, meetings, reading, coding, or explanations.

Simple explanation

When you say **“where we left off”**, you are referring to **the exact point where you stopped earlier**.

**Example:**

* “Let’s continue from where we left off yesterday.”  
   This means: let’s start again from the same place we stopped yesterday.

You can use it in:

* meetings  
* presentations  
* coding discussions  
* storytelling  
* learning sessions  
* casual conversations

Common spoken sentences you can use 🎯

Try practicing these aloud:

1. “Let’s pick up where we left off in the last meeting.”  
2. “I think we left off at the deployment step.”  
3. “Can we continue from where we left off yesterday?”  
4. “Last time, we left off while discussing the architecture changes.”  
5. “So, just to recap, we left off at the data preprocessing stage.”

Notice the connectors:

* **last time**  
* **so**  
* **just to recap**  
* **I think**  
   These make your speech sound natural and structured.

Usage in IT / architecture discussions 💻

Since you often explain technical workflows (for example, pipelines, deployments, or MLOps stages), this phrase is very useful:

* “Previously, we left off at the model training phase; today we’ll move to deployment.”  
* “As far as I remember, we left off after setting up the cluster configuration.”

Similar expressions (use interchangeably)

* “pick up from where we left off”  
* “continue from the last point”  
* “resume from there”  
* “carry on from the previous step”

Example comparison:

“Let’s resume from the last point” sounds slightly formal.  
 “Let’s pick up where we left off” sounds natural in meetings.

Quick speaking practice exercise 🗣️

Say this aloud:

“Last time we left off at the data ingestion step. Today, I’d like to continue from there and walk you through the transformation layer.”

This sentence is excellent for presentations and client calls.

# Left out

**1\. *Left out*** ✅ (most common & natural)  
 Use this when others are engaged, but you don’t feel included.

*“I felt left out because the conversation wasn’t something I could relate to.”*

**2\. *Out of place*** ✅  
 When you’re physically present but mentally disconnected.

*“I felt out of place in that group discussion.”*

**3\. *Disconnected***  
 A softer, more internal feeling — not emotional rejection, just no mental connection.

*“I felt disconnected from the conversation.”*

**4\. *Alienated*** (stronger, heavier)  
 Use this if it feels repeated or emotionally uncomfortable.

*“I felt alienated because the topic was completely outside my world.”*

**5\. *Invisible*** (emotional, expressive)  
 When no one notices your silence.

*“I felt invisible in that group.”*

**6\. *Detached***  
 More neutral, almost observational.

*“I felt detached from the discussion.”*

### **Words that are *close* but slightly off**

* **Isolated** → usually implies being *separated or alone*, even physically  
  c  
* **Lost** → more about confusion than exclusion

They’re not wrong, just less precise for this scenario.

### **Best one-line, natural sentence**

If you want something that sounds fluent and native:

**“I felt a bit left out and disconnected since the conversation was beyond my interest.”**

Or more casual:

**“I was there, but I felt out of place.”**

phrasal verb of *leave*

adjective: **left out** — fail to include someone or something. "It seemed unkind to leave Daisy out, so she was invited too." Also used as an adjective meaning excluded: "Not knowing the language I felt really left out."

Senses:
- Excluded or omitted: for example, you might proofread carefully to catch left-out quotation marks or parentheses. You might also feel left out if you don't know the language and are excluded from something.
- Remaining behind in a visible place: for example, raccoons might eat pet food that's been left out.
- Unhappy because not included: for example, you might feel left out if your friends talk about sports and you're not included.

# Lock in

“Lock in” has a few different meanings depending on the situation:

1. **General meaning**: To secure or confirm something so it cannot be changed.

   * Example: *“We locked in the price before it increased.”*

2. **Business/work**: To finalize a deal, schedule, or agreement.

   * Example: *“Let’s lock in the meeting time for tomorrow.”*

3. **Casual/slang**: To focus seriously or commit fully to something.

   * Example: *“I need to lock in and finish this project.”*

4. **Finance/investing**: To secure a rate or profit.

   * Example: *“He locked in his gains before the market dipped.”*

### **Phrases you can use in real conversations:**

* “Let’s lock in the plan so everyone is on the same page.”

* “I’ve locked in my schedule for next week, so I can confirm.”

* “If we lock in the price today, we’ll save money later.”

* “I really need to lock in and focus on this task.”

Commit permanently.

# Look Through

**1\. Meaning in general spoken English**

In casual conversation, **"look through"** usually has these main senses:

1. **To quickly read or examine something**

   * *"I’ll look through your report tonight."* (means skim or review it)

   * *"Can you look through these notes and find the key points?"*

2. **To search inside something for something specific**

   * *"I looked through my bag for my keys."*

3. **To ignore someone deliberately** (less common)

   * *"She looked right through me."* (acted like she didn’t see me)

**2\. Usage in spoken English**

In natural speech, "look through" is often:

* **Informal and quick** when talking about checking documents or items.

* Often combined with **pronouns** like *look through it / look through them*.

* Frequently used with **time markers**: *"I’ll look through it later"* or *"Just look through these before the meeting."*

**3\. Usage in a presentation about AI**

If you’re presenting about AI, **"look through"** works well when describing:

* **Reviewing datasets or documents**

  * *"We looked through thousands of medical records to train the AI."*

* **Scanning information quickly**

  * *"The algorithm can look through millions of images in seconds."*

* **Checking results or models**

  * *"Our team looked through the output to ensure accuracy."*

⚠ In presentations, "look through" sounds **casual**, so for a professional tone you might sometimes replace it with:

* *review*

* *examine*

* *analyze*

* *go through* (still informal but common in tech talks)

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Look up

To search something in dictionary/ book

He was looking up the book for a sentence

Im looking up a word in the dictionary

### **1\. Look up / Looked up**

**Meaning:**

* *Look up* (phrasal verb) means to **search for information** (often in a book, database, or online).

* *Looked up* is the past tense.

**Examples:**

* *“I need to look up the address of the new office.”*

* *“She looked up the definition in the dictionary.”*

* *“Can you look up the status of the Databricks job?”*

* *“He looked up the error code to find a solution.”*

### **2\. Lookup (noun or adjective)**

* *Lookup* (one word) is usually a **noun** or an **adjective** referring to the **process or action of searching** for information.

* Common in computing or databases: a *lookup table* or *lookup operation* means using a key to find data.

* *“The program performs a fast lookup to retrieve user details.”*

* *“We use a lookup table to map user IDs to email addresses.”*

* *“During the pipeline, a lookup step validates the input data.”*

### **Look up / Looked up (verb phrases)**

* *“I looked up the latest cloud security best practices before finalizing the architecture.”*

* *“You can look up the incident history in the AIOps platform to identify recurring issues.”*

* *“During the design review, I looked up how our cloud provider handles autoscaling policies.”*

### **Lookup (noun or adjective)**

* *“We implemented a lookup table to map cloud resource IDs to their owners for better audit tracking.”*

* *“The AIOps system performs a fast lookup of log patterns to detect anomalies in real time.”*

* *“Our monitoring pipeline includes a lookup step that enriches alerts with metadata from the CMDB.”*

# Make an impression on

# Make out

The phrase **“make out”** can mean different things depending on the context. Let me break it down simply:

1. **Understand / manage to see or hear clearly**

   * *“I couldn’t make out what he was saying because of the noise.”*

   * *“Can you make out that sign in the distance?”*

2. **Pretend / claim**

   * *“He makes himself out to be an expert, but he’s not.”*

3. **Progress / manage**

   * *“How are you making out in your new job?”* (means: how are you managing/doing)

4. **Romantic/physical meaning** (informal, casual)

   * *“They were making out in the car.”* (kissing or being intimate)

✅ **Useful sentences for conversation practice**:

* “I couldn’t make out what you just said, could you repeat?”

* “From far away, I couldn’t make out the details.”

* “He makes it out like it was all my fault.”

* “How are you making out with the project so far?”

👉 Try practicing aloud with connecting phrases:

* “On the other hand, I could make out some words even though the call wasn’t clear.”

* “For example, sometimes people make themselves out to be more confident than they really are.”

* “That being said, it’s not always easy to make out handwriting in old documents.”

# Moment of Truth

The phrase **“moment of truth”** means the point in time when something important is tested, revealed, or decided. It’s that critical moment when you find out whether something works, succeeds, or fails.

For example:

* In sports, the penalty kick in the final seconds can be called *the moment of truth*.

* In work, when you finally present your solution to the client, that’s *the moment of truth*.

* In personal life, confessing your feelings to someone can also feel like *the moment of truth*.

It usually carries a sense of pressure and finality—where preparation ends, and reality shows the result.

✅ Here are some **spoken phrases** you could use in real life:

1. **“This is the moment of truth—let’s see if our hard work pays off.”**

2. **“We’ve done all the preparation; now comes the moment of truth.”**

3. **“It’s the moment of truth for our project presentation.”**

4. **“The moment of truth will show whether the system can handle real traffic.”**

# moving forward

### **✅ Meaning of “moving forward”**

The phrase **“moving forward”** means *from this point on* or *in the future*.  
 It’s often used in **spoken English** to shift focus toward **what will happen next** or **what should be done next** — especially in meetings or discussions.

### **✅ Examples in spoken English**

1. **“Moving forward, we should document all our deployments.”**  
    → (From now on, we’ll do this every time.)

2. **“Let’s try to improve our testing strategy moving forward.”**  
    → (In the future, we should make it better.)

3. **“Moving forward, we need to avoid the same mistake.”**  
    → (Going ahead, let’s not repeat it.)

4. **“I think moving forward, we’ll face some new challenges.”**  
    → (In the future, there may be new issues.)

### **⚠️ Your example:**

“Moving forward in further we can get this as issue as well.”

That sentence is understandable but **not natural**.  
 Here are better ways to say it:

* ✅ “Moving forward, we might face this issue again.”

* ✅ “We could run into this issue again moving forward.”

* ✅ “This could become a recurring issue moving forward.”

### **💬 Common spoken phrases you can practice:**

1. “Moving forward, I suggest we keep things more transparent.”

2. “That being said, we’ll have to monitor this closely moving forward.”

3. “On the other hand, moving forward, we can plan better to avoid it.”

4. “For example, if this happens again moving forward, we’ll be prepared.”

5. “So, moving forward, let’s make sure we’re aligned on this.”

# Needle back

**“Needle back”** is usually used in the context of **responding to someone who is teasing, provoking, or criticizing you**. It comes from the verb **“needle”**, which means **to annoy, provoke, or goad someone deliberately**.

* Often playful, sometimes defensive, depending on tone.

### **Usage in Spoken English:**

1. **Playful teasing:**

   * *“He kept making jokes about my cooking, so I needled back about his messy kitchen.”*

2. **Friendly banter:**

3. **Figurative / mild conflict:**

* “Needling” is more **informal** and **conversational**, often used in **friendly teasing** or **light arguments**.

* You can say: *“I couldn’t help needling back”* or *“He needled me, so I needled back.”*

# Off guard

### **Off guard — Meaning**

**“Off guard”** means **being surprised or unprepared for something**.  
 When someone is *caught off guard*, it means they **did not expect the situation and were not ready to respond immediately**.

So, the idea is: **you were not mentally prepared.**

For example:  
 If someone suddenly asks you a difficult question in a meeting and you were not expecting it, you might feel **caught off guard**.

### **Simple Examples (Real-life usage)**

1. **Meeting / Work**  
* *“The client asked a question about pricing and it caught me off guard.”*  
2. **Conversation**  
* *“His comment really caught me off guard. I didn’t know how to respond.”*  
3. **Unexpected situation**  
* *“The sudden change in the plan caught everyone off guard.”*  
4. **Personal reaction**  
* *“I was caught off guard when she mentioned my name in the presentation.”*

### **Important Phrase**

The most common expression is:

**“caught off guard”**

* *“I was caught off guard by that question.”*

### **Comparison with similar words**

| Phrase | Meaning |
| ----- | ----- |
| **Off guard** | Not prepared |
| **Surprised** | Unexpected reaction |
| **Taken aback** | Emotionally surprised |
| **Caught off guard** | Suddenly unprepared |

Example difference:

* *“I was surprised by the question.”*  
* *“I was caught off guard by the question.”* → stronger; shows **lack of preparation**

### **Useful Spoken Sentences (Practice for meetings)**

Try speaking these aloud:

1. **“That question caught me off guard, but let me think about it for a moment.”**  
2. **“To be honest, that came a bit out of nowhere and caught me off guard.”**  
3. **“Initially it caught me off guard; however, after thinking about it, the solution became clear.”**  
4. **“For example, when unexpected questions come in a meeting, they can sometimes catch you off guard.”**  
5. **“That being said, it’s always good to pause and organize your thoughts before answering.”**

### **How you might use it in your real scenario**

Since you mentioned earlier that **sometimes in client calls you struggle when unexpected questions come**, you could say something like:

*“That question caught me slightly off guard. Let me quickly walk through the architecture and explain how it works.”*

This sounds **natural and professional in meetings**.

### **Quick Speaking Practice**

Say this sentence aloud 3 times:

**“The question caught me off guard, but let me think through it and explain.”**

This will help you sound **calm and confident even when surprised**.

If you'd like, I can also show you **5 advanced phrases similar to “caught off guard” that sound very natural in meetings.** (These are extremely useful in professional conversations.)

# On and on

The phrase **“on and on”** means *something continues without stopping, often for a long time, and sometimes in an annoying or boring way*.  
 It’s commonly used in spoken English when describing:

* Speech that doesn’t stop

* Actions or processes that keep repeating

* Time that feels endless

**Examples in Spoken English**

1. **About talking:**

   * *“He went on and on about his new car, and honestly, it got a bit tiring.”*  
      (Here, it means he kept talking too much.)

2. **About time/duration:**

   * *“The meeting went on and on, and we were all waiting for it to finish.”*  
      (The meeting lasted much longer than expected.)

3. **About effort or action:**

   * *“She kept working on and on until the project was finally done.”*  
      (She continued without stopping.)

Connected Sentences (how to link smoothly)

* *“The lecture went on and on, **and to make matters worse**, the speaker didn’t even take questions.”*

* *“He kept talking on and on about his vacation, **but at the same time**, it was nice to see his excitement.”*

* *“The rain went on and on, **so eventually**, the match had to be canceled.”*

Phrases you can practice saying in meetings or casual talk

* “This discussion can’t just go on and on, we need to decide.”

* “The issue has dragged on and on without resolution.”

* “She explained the details on and on, until everyone understood.”

* “It felt like the movie just went on and on.”

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# On Him

### **1\. “On him / on her” with phrasal verbs**

* Many English verbs take a preposition, and the preposition cannot be dropped.

* For example:

  * *“I count on him.”* → means *I rely on him.*

  * *“She depends on her.”* → means *She trusts her for support.*

Here, **“on” is part of the verb (count on / depend on)**, and “him/her” is the object.

### **2\. When “on” shows direction / position**

Sometimes “on him / on her” literally means physically “on top of” or “attached to”:

* *“The jacket looks good on him.”* → The jacket suits him.

* *“The baby is sleeping on her.”* → Baby is lying on her body.

### **3\. When it shows responsibility / blame**

* *“That’s on him.”* → It means *That’s his responsibility or his fault.*

* *“Don’t blame me, it’s on her.”* → The fault is hers.

✅ **So, the pattern is not a special grammar rule by itself**. It usually comes from either:

* A phrasal verb (*count on, rely on, depend on*),

* A descriptive phrase (*looks good on him*),

* Or an idiom (*that’s on him \= his responsibility*).

### **Phrases you can practice saying:**

1. *“You can always count on me.”* (phrasal verb use)

2. *“That shirt looks perfect on her.”* (appearance use)

3. *“If the project fails, that’s on him.”* (responsibility use)

4. *“She’s depending on him to finish the work.”* (trust/support use)  
5. “He doesn’t have a keyboard on him.”  
6. *“Do you have your ID on you?”* (Do you have it with you?)

7. *“She didn’t have any cash on her.”* (She wasn’t carrying cash.)

8. *“They don’t have their tickets on them.”* (They don’t have the tickets with them right now.)  
9. *“Sorry, I don’t have any money on me right now.”*

10. *“Do you have a pen on you?”*

11. *“He didn’t have his phone on him when I called.”*

12. *“She always has her keys on her.”*  
13. *“He gave me feedback **on** my presentation.”* → feedback **about** the presentation.

14. *“She wrote a review **on** the new book.”* → review **about** the book.

15. *“Can you give me comments **on** my report?”* → comments **about** the report.

16. *“The manager’s remarks **on** the project were helpful.”* → remarks **about** the project.

    ### **💡 Compare with other prepositions:**

* **“On”** → about the topic. (*review on the movie*)

* **“About”** → also works, but **“on” sounds more formal and natural** in work/academic settings.

* **“For”** → different meaning. *“Feedback for me”* \= directed to me, not necessarily about my work.

  ### **Spoken phrases you can practice:**

1. *“Can you share your comments **on** this document?”*

2. *“I’d like to get your feedback **on** my code.”*

3. *“The client left a review **on** our website.”*

4. *“He gave some helpful notes **on** my presentation.”*

"On paper, the two services are decoupled — in practice, they still share a database."

# on the flip side

The phrase **“on the flip side”** is a casual, conversational way to say **“on the other hand”** or **“from another perspective.”** It’s used when you want to show contrast or present a different point of view.

### **Usage in Spoken English:**

1. **To show the opposite side of an argument or situation:**

   * *“I love living in the city. On the flip side, it’s really noisy.”*

   * *“Working from home is convenient. On the flip side, it can be lonely.”*

2. **To give a contrasting result or consequence:**

   * *“The new policy saves money. On the flip side, it makes employees’ jobs harder.”*

3. **To soften disagreement or criticism:**

   * *“Your idea is interesting. On the flip side, we might need to consider the costs.”*

💡 **Tip:**

* It’s **informal**, so you’ll mostly hear it in **conversation**, not formal writing.

* Can be swapped with **“however”**, **“alternatively”**, or **“but”**, depending on context.

# Out of the way

The phrase "out of the way" can have several meanings depending on the context. Here are the most common ones:

**Not obstructing or blocking:**

Something or someone is no longer in a position that causes an obstacle.  
Example: "He moved the chair out of the way so I could pass."

**Completed or dealt with:**

A task or responsibility has been finished or resolved.  
Example: "Let’s get this project out of the way before starting a new one."

**In a remote or isolated location:**

Refers to a place that is not easily accessible or is far from the main areas.  
Example: "Their house is in a quiet, out-of-the-way village."

**Unusual or extraordinary (less common usage):**

Refers to something that is not normal or expected.  
Example: "It was out of the way for him to act so kindly."

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Over the top

"A little over the top" is an idiom that means something is slightly too extreme or excessive for the situation. For example, you might describe a birthday party with clowns, ponies, and a chocolate fountain as "over the top". 

Examples 

"I thought her performance was a little over the top".

"The decorations were a little over the top".

"The speech was a bit OTT".

Synonyms for "over the top": 

excessive, extravagant, extreme, insane, inappropriate, unbearable, flamboyant, outrageous, ridiculous, and unbelievable.

**"Over the top"** means **excessive**, **exaggerated**, or **beyond what’s reasonable or normal**.  
 It can describe behavior, emotions, style, or actions — sometimes in a negative way (too much) and sometimes in a playful or admiring way (dramatic flair).

1. **Negative / Excessive**

   * *"His reaction to the mistake was over the top — he yelled for an hour."*

   * *"The decorations were beautiful but a bit over the top for a small party."*

2. **Positive / Playful or Dramatic**

   * *"The performance was over the top, but that’s what made it so entertaining."*

   * *"She wore an over-the-top costume for Halloween and stole the show."*

### **🔸 Synonyms:**

* Excessive

* Exaggerated

* Extreme

* Dramatic

💡 In **military history**, "over the top" originally meant soldiers going out of their trenches to attack — but in everyday English today, it’s almost always about **exaggeration**.

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# paint the picture

The phrase **“paint the picture”** is an idiom. It doesn’t mean to literally paint with colors—it means to **describe something clearly and vividly so that the listener can imagine it in their mind**.

For example, in a meeting, your manager might say:  
 👉 *“Can you paint the picture for us about where the project stands right now?”*  
 That means they want you to explain the situation in detail so everyone can see the “whole scene” mentally.

It’s often used in business, storytelling, or personal conversations when someone wants a fuller explanation instead of short facts.

### **Useful sentences you can practice:**

1. *“Let me paint the picture for you—this is where we started and this is where we are now.”*

2. *“To paint the picture more clearly, imagine if…”*

3. *“Before I explain the solution, I’ll paint the picture of the problem first.”*

4. *“That paints a pretty good picture of the challenges we’re facing.”*

💡 **Context of usage**:

* In **meetings** (explaining status or challenges).

* In **presentations** (helping the audience visualize data or a scenario).

* In **casual talk** (telling a story, describing an event, setting the scene).

# Pan out

**"Pan out"** means *to turn out well* or *to happen in a particular way*, especially when talking about the outcome of plans, ideas, or efforts.

**Examples:**

* *We’ll see how the project pans out before making a decision.*

* *His risky investment didn’t pan out.* (it didn’t succeed)

* *I hope things pan out for you in your new job.*

**Origin:**  
 It comes from gold prospecting — miners would swirl a pan of river sediment hoping to find gold. If the gold appeared, the effort “panned out” successfully.

It’s mostly used in **informal spoken English** today.

To turn out or happen successfully.  
 👉 *“Let’s see how this plan pans out.”*

# Pent up

“**Pent up**” means something that has been **held inside or suppressed for a long time**, usually emotions, energy, or feelings, and then it either builds pressure or comes out strongly later.

* If someone has **pent-up anger**, it means they’ve been holding their anger in without expressing it.

* If you have **pent-up energy**, it means you’ve been sitting idle and now you feel restless and want to move.

It comes from the idea of something being “penned up” (like animals in a pen) — trapped and unable to get out.

✅ **Examples in sentences (spoken style):**

1. “He finally shouted after days of keeping his **pent-up frustration** inside.”

2. “Kids have so much **pent-up energy** after sitting in class all day.”

3. “You could tell she had **pent-up emotions**—once she started talking, she just couldn’t stop.”

4. “I think I need to go for a run; I’ve got a lot of **pent-up energy**.”

💡 **Where you can use it:**

* In **work meetings**, if talking about stress: *“We shouldn’t let **pent-up frustration** affect the team.”*

* In **casual talk**, about kids or friends: *“They had so much **pent-up energy** after being indoors.”*

* In **personal reflection**, about emotions: *“I realized I had a lot of **pent-up feelings** I needed to express.”*

👉 Try speaking aloud:

* “I think I have a lot of pent-up stress these days.”

* “After the exam, all my pent-up tension just disappeared.”

adjective  
1\.  
(of emotions, energy, etc.) unable to be expressed or released.  
"pent-up frustrations"  
2\.  
closely confined or held back.  
"a surge of pent-up water"

Take out your pent up feelings.

# pipe dream

A **“pipe dream”** means a hope, plan, or idea that is unrealistic or unlikely to happen.
