# was not the thing yet

the phrase **“was not the thing yet”** is a casual or idiomatic way of saying that *something wasn’t popular, common, or established at that time*.

Let’s break it down 👇

### **🔹 Meaning**

When someone says:

“X was not the thing yet,”  
 they mean that *X wasn’t trendy, known, or widely used at that time.*

It’s like saying:

* “X hadn’t become popular yet.”

* “X didn’t exist or wasn’t mainstream yet.”

### **🔹 Examples in Different Scenarios**

1. **Technology context**

   * “When I started programming, *AI was not the thing yet.* People were still focused on web development.”  
      👉 Meaning: AI wasn’t as popular or mainstream as it is today.

2. **Cultural context**

   * “Back in the early 2000s, *social media was not the thing yet.* We used to chat on MSN Messenger.”  
      👉 Meaning: Social media hadn’t become a trend yet.

3. **Fashion context**

   * “When I was in college, *streetwear was not the thing yet.* Everyone dressed quite formally.”  
      👉 Meaning: Streetwear wasn’t fashionable or widely accepted then.

4. **Workplace context**

   * “Remote work *was not the thing yet* before COVID-19.”  
      👉 Meaning: People didn’t commonly work from home before the pandemic.

### **🔹 Common Spoken Alternatives**

Here are some other phrases you can use with the same idea:

* “That wasn’t popular back then.”

* “It hadn’t caught on yet.”

* “That trend hadn’t started yet.”

* “It wasn’t mainstream at the time.”

### **🔹 Practice Sentences (for speaking)**

Try saying these aloud to get comfortable:

1. “Cloud computing *was not the thing yet* when I started my career.”

2. “Streaming services *were not the thing yet* — we used to buy DVDs.”

3. “Data science *hadn’t caught on yet* when I graduated.”

4. “Back then, working remotely *wasn’t a thing.*”

🗣️ **Tip:** Practice saying it with a natural tone — the phrase “not the thing yet” usually carries a *nostalgic* or *reflective* tone.

# Weigh In

**"Weigh in"** is a phrasal verb that means **to give your opinion, advice, or input on a matter** — especially when a discussion or decision is happening.

### **Meaning in spoken English**

* **Literal origin:** from boxing or sports, where athletes are weighed before competing.

* **Figurative modern meaning:** *join the conversation and share your thoughts or judgment.*

### **Examples**

1. *"We’re deciding which framework to use — can you weigh in?"*

2. *"I’d like to weigh in on the design before we finalize it."*

3. *"Several experts weighed in on the debate."*

💡 **Workplace use:**

* Often used in meetings or group chats when you want someone’s **technical opinion**.

* Can be polite: *"Feel free to weigh in if you have suggestions."*

* Or assertive: *"I want to weigh in here — I think we should refactor the code."*

**Weighing a contestant**  
The act of weighing an athlete, such as a boxer or jockey, before a competition. For example, "The boxers arrived for the pre-fight weigh-in".   
**Contributing to a discussion**  
To join a discussion, argument, or activity by saying something important, persuading someone, or doing something to help. For example, "We all weighed in with our suggestions".   
**Bringing weight or influence to bear**  
To bring one's weight or influence to bear, especially as a participant, contributor, or mediator. For example, "He weighed in with a few sharp comments"

# Work on

* It means **to focus your effort on something**, usually to improve it, develop it, or complete it.

* It’s the **standard, modern, natural phrase** in both casual and professional English.

* Example: *“I’m working on a new presentation.”* (You’re preparing it.)

* Example: *“She’s working on her English.”* (She’s improving her English skills.)

So whenever you want to talk about tasks, skills, projects, or self-improvement → use **work on**.

### **❌ Why not “work upon”?**

1. **Old-fashioned / Formal** – “Work upon” sounds like something from the 18th or 19th century. Modern English has shifted to “work on.”

   * Example: *“The preacher’s words worked upon their emotions.”* (literary, not everyday speech).

2. **Different nuance** – “Work upon” often means **to influence or have an effect on someone**, not literally doing a task.

   * Example: *“The idea worked upon his mind until he finally acted.”*

3. **Unnatural in conversation** – If you say *“I’m working upon a project”* in a meeting, it will sound odd or too formal. Native speakers almost always say *“working on a project.”*

👉 In short: **use “work on” for everyday tasks/projects/skills.** Reserve “work upon” only if you’re writing old-style literature or want to sound poetic.

### **Spoken examples you can practice:**

1. *“I’m working on a new dashboard design for the client.”*

2. *“On the other hand, I also want to work on my public speaking skills.”*

3. *“That being said, I’m still working on fixing the issue.”*

4. *“Let’s work on this problem together and find a solution.”*

💡 **Tip for meetings:** Whenever you’re tempted to say *“work upon”*, just replace it with *“work on”*. That’s what sounds natural in modern English.

# Write Off / written off

The phrase "written off" has various meanings depending on the context:

Financial Context:

Meaning: To cancel a debt because it is unlikely to be paid or recoverable.  
Example: "The bank wrote off the loan as bad debt after repeated defaults."  
Insurance Context:

Meaning: When an asset, such as a car, is deemed damaged beyond repair or its repair cost exceeds its value.  
Example: "The car was written off after the accident because the repairs were too expensive."  
General Context:

Meaning: To disregard or dismiss someone or something as unimportant, hopeless, or a failure.  
Example: "He was written off as a failure, but he proved everyone wrong by succeeding in his career."

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# yank out

The phrase **“yank out”** means to pull something out suddenly and forcefully.  
 It’s informal and often used when the action is quick, strong, or even a bit rough.

### **Examples:**

* *He yanked out the weeds from the garden.*

* *The dentist yanked out my tooth.*

* *She yanked out the USB drive without ejecting it properly.*

So it carries the idea of **not gently removing**, but rather pulling quickly with strength.

### **Useful spoken phrases with “yank out”:**

1. *“He just yanked out the plug without asking.”*

2. *“I had to yank out the drawer because it was stuck.”*

3. *“She yanked out her phone to show me the picture.”*

4. *“The machine stopped working when someone yanked out the cable.”*

👉 You can practice saying these aloud.  
 By the way, in professional or polite conversation, instead of *“yank out,”* people often say **“pull out,” “unplug,” or “remove.”**

# you are way out of line

The phrase "you are way out of line" is used to tell someone they are behaving inappropriately or crossing boundaries, often in terms of respect, rules, or acceptable behavior. Here's a breakdown of its use:

Situations Where It's Used

Breaking Rules or Protocols  
"Ignoring safety regulations like that is way out of line."

 **Examples (spoken):**

* “You stay as long as it takes to finish the job.”

* “Don’t rush — stay as long as it takes.”

* “We’ll wait; you stay as long as it takes.”

👉 *Tone:* Supportive and understanding.

🗣 *“You bump into me again and you’ll see — you want a piece of me?”*  
 🗣 *“He shouted, ‘You want a piece of me?\!’ after the insult.”*

To focus sharply on something.

# Zero Sum Game

A Zero-Sum Game is a situation in which one person's gain is exactly balanced by another person's loss. The total amount of value remains the same—one wins, the other loses.

**Examples**:  
Poker: If one player wins $100, another player (or players) must have lost $100 in total.  
Stock Trading: If a trader profits from a short sale, someone else on the other side of the trade takes the loss.  
**Non-Zero-Sum Example:**  
Business Collaboration: Two companies partnering to create a new product benefit both, instead of one winning at the other’s expense.

## Zero-sum

**adjective**  
relating to or denoting a situation in which whatever is gained by one side is lost by the other.  
"altruism is not a zero-sum game"

To see the bigger picture by stepping back from the details.

"Let's zoom out from this one bug and ask whether the whole approach is right."
"Zooming out from the individual incidents, there's a clear pattern in what's failing."

# “overspill” and “spill over”

Both **“overspill”** and **“spill over”** can be used with water, but they are used in *different ways* and are *not interchangeable*.

Let me explain clearly and simply.

## **✅ Correct Usage**

### **1\. Spill over (phrasal verb)**

Meaning:  
 Water flows over the edge because the container is full.

**Used for action (verb).**

**Examples (water context):**

* “The tank started to **spill over** because the inlet valve was stuck.”  
* “If you keep filling the bucket, the water will **spill over** the rim.”  
* “The river **spilled over** into the nearby fields due to heavy rains.”

### **2\. Overspill (noun)**

Meaning:  
 The extra amount of water that has spilled.

**Used as a noun (thing).**

**Examples:**

* “There was a lot of **overspill** from the tank.”  
* “The drainage system handles the water **overspill** during monsoon.”

You **don’t say**: “The water overspilled.” (Incorrect as a verb)

## **⭐ Simple rule to remember**

* **Action \= spill over** (verb)  
* **Extra water \= overspill** (noun)

So in conversation, you’ll most often use **spill over**.

## **🗣️ Useful spoken sentences you can practice**

Try saying these aloud:

1. “The tank was almost full, and the water began to **spill over**, so I switched off the pump.”  
2. “During the storm, the lake **spilled over** into the nearby pathway.”  
3. “We need a better drainage system to handle the **overspill**.”  
4. “On the other hand, if we manage the flow properly, we can avoid any **spill over**.”  
5. “For example, open reservoirs often **spill over** after continuous rainfall.”

## **🎤 Practice Tip**

Speak these sentences slowly first, then again with natural speed. Focus on connecting words like *“on the other hand,” “for example,”* etc. These make your speech smoother.

# “reflect on” and “reflect upon”

Both **“reflect on”** and **“reflect upon”** are correct English, and in most situations they mean the **same thing**. The difference is more about **style and tone**, not meaning.

## **Reflect on**

✅ **More common in modern, everyday English**  
 ✅ Sounds **natural, simple, and conversational**  
 ✅ Preferred in both **spoken English** and **professional writing**

**Examples**

* *I need some time to **reflect on** what you said.*  
* *She reflected on her past experiences.*  
* *Let’s reflect on today’s meeting.*

👉 This is the form you should **default to**.

## **Reflect upon**

✅ Slightly **more formal or literary**  
 ✅ Often used in **academic writing, essays, or philosophical contexts**  
 ❌ Less common in daily speech

* *He reflected upon the meaning of success.*  
* *The author reflects upon human suffering.*

👉 Sounds more **old-fashioned or thoughtful**, but not wrong.

## **Do native speakers distinguish them?**

Not really. Native speakers **don’t consciously choose** one for meaning—only for **tone**.

## **Quick rule (easy to remember)**

* **Speaking / meetings / emails** → **reflect on**  
* **Essays / philosophy / literature** → **reflect upon**

### **💡 Since you’re actively improving your spoken English:**

Use **“reflect on”** in conversation.  
 It will sound **natural, fluent, and native-like**.

# “TL;DR” means “Too Long; Didn’t Read.”

It's an internet shorthand people use when a text, email, or article is very long, and someone wants to give a **short summary** of it. You’ll often see it at the end of a post, where the writer gives a quick version of the main point.

For example:

* *“I wrote a long blog about my trip. TL;DR: It was amazing, but exhausting.”*

* *“TL;DR: The project deadline is extended by one week.”*

### **Phrases you can practice in real conversations:**

1. *“The article was too long; TL;DR, the new policy starts next month.”*

2. *“I’ll give you the TL;DR: we need to focus on automation first.”*

3. *“TL;DR, the meeting went well and we got approval.”*

4. *“That was a lot of information; can you give me the TL;DR version?”*

👉 Context of usage:

* **Workplace:** summarizing long reports or emails.

* **Casual talk:** when explaining a long story quickly.

* **Online forums/chats:** when replying to long posts.

✨ I’d suggest practicing saying: *“TL;DR means a short summary.”* Try saying it aloud as if you’re explaining it to a colleague.

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# “We have other things to do.”

Still, in everyday conversation, especially in American English, people often say **“We got other things to do”** to mean the same thing—it just sounds more relaxed and less formal.

You’d use it when you want to politely cut something short or shift focus, for example:

* Wrapping up a meeting

* Telling a friend you can’t waste time on something trivial

* Indicating you need to move on to another task

✅ Here are some natural sentences you can practice:

1. *“Let’s wrap this up, we got other things to do.”*

2. *“I wish I could stay longer, but I got other things to do.”*

3. *“We can’t spend all day on this, we got other things to do.”*

4. *“It’s been great talking, but I got other things to do right now.”*

👉 Notice how I used connecting phrases like *“but”* and *“let’s wrap this up”* to make the sentences flow.

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# 🔹 Other Contexts Where It’s Used

* Emotions: fear, doubt, jealousy, anxiety  
* Business: risk, inefficiency, delays  
* Technology: bugs, latency, data corruption  
* Health: infection, fatigue  
* Relationships: misunderstandings

## **💡 Small Pronunciation Tip**

It’s pronounced:

**“kreep in”** (long “ee” sound)

Not “crip in.”

## **1\. “Let me catch you up”**

### **✅ Meaning**

It means: *“Let me update you on what has happened so far.”*

👉 Very common in meetings when someone joined late or missed context.

### **🔧 Slight improvement**

Your sentence is correct, but more natural:

* **“Let me catch you up on this.”**  
* **“Let me quickly catch you up.”**

### **💬 Example**

* “Let me catch you up — we already deployed the first version, but we’re facing some issues in RAE.”

### **🗣 Practice Sentences**

* “Let me catch you up on the current status.”  
* “Before we move ahead, let me catch you up.”  
* “Just to bring you up to speed, here’s what happened.”

## **2\. “I have a rudimentary question”**

### **⚠️ Correction**

You wrote: *“rudamentary”* → correct spelling is **rudimentary**

It means: *a very basic or fundamental question*

👉 But in spoken English, this can sound slightly formal or even self-diminishing.

### **👍 Better alternatives (more natural in meetings)**

* “I have a basic question”  
* “This might be a simple question, but…”  
* “Just a quick question…”

* “I have a basic question — how is RAE triggered in this flow?”

* “This might be a simple question, but can you clarify this part?”  
* “I just have a quick question here.”  
* “Sorry if this sounds basic, but I want to confirm something.”

## **3\. “In regards to RAE or in the first place?”**

### **⚠️ Needs restructuring (this is not very natural as is)**

### **What you *probably* want to say:**

You are trying to compare:

* Is this related to RAE?  
* Or was it like this from the beginning?

### **✅ Better natural versions:**

* “Is this specific to RAE, or was it like this from the beginning?”  
* “Is this happening because of RAE, or was it always like this?”  
* “Is this related to RAE, or is it a general behavior?”

* “Is this issue tied to RAE, or does it exist independently?”  
* “On the other hand, was this already the case earlier?”  
* “For example, was this behavior present before RAE changes?”

## **4\. “Has this always been off?”**

### **⚠️ Slight improvement needed**

“off” is vague unless context is very clear.

### **✅ Better versions:**

* “Has this always been disabled?”  
* “Was this always turned off?”  
* “Has this been off from the beginning?”

* “Has this always been disabled, or was it changed recently?”

* “Was this setting always off?”  
* “As far as I remember, this was enabled earlier — did something change?”  
* “That being said, has this been off all along?”

## **5\. “Let’s switch over to this topic”**

Changing focus to another topic

### **👍 More natural options:**

* “Let’s switch to this topic.” (more common)  
* “Let’s move on to this.”  
* “Let’s shift focus to this.”

* “Let’s switch to this topic — the RAE execution flow.”

* “Now, let’s move on to the next point.”  
* “Coming back to this topic…”  
* “Alright, let’s shift focus here.”

## **🔥 Combined Natural Flow (Very Useful for Meetings)**

Try saying this aloud:

“Let me quickly catch you up. We’ve already processed the batches.  
 Now, I have a basic question — is this behavior specific to RAE, or was it always like this?  
 That being said, has this been disabled from the beginning?  
 Anyway, let’s switch to the next topic.”

## **🎯 Speaking Tip**

You already think clearly — your challenge is **structuring thoughts smoothly**.

👉 Focus on connectors:

* “Now,”  
* “That being said,”  
* “On the other hand,”  
* “Just to clarify,”

## **✅ Small Correction in Your Original Sentence**

Your sentence:

“i have rudamentary question”

Better:  
 👉 **“I have a rudimentary question.”**  
 👉 Even better (spoken): **“I have a quick/basic question.”**

Say this out loud 2–3 times:

## **1\. “It never made it way to production.”**

👉 It should be:  
 **“It never made its way to production.”**

Something was planned or developed, but **never got deployed/released**.

### **💬 Natural usage**

* “The fix was ready, but it never made its way to production.”

* “This change never made it to production.”  
* “For some reason, it didn’t go live.”  
* “It was implemented, but it never got deployed.”

## **2\. “Where are you at?”**

This means:

* What’s your current **status/progress**?  
* Where are you in the **process**?

👉 Very common in informal meetings.

### **👍 Slightly more professional options**

* “Where are you with this?”  
* “What’s the current status?”  
* “Can you share where things stand?”

* “Where are you with the debugging so far?”  
* “Can you give a quick status update?”  
* “As of now, where do we stand on this issue?”

## **3\. “You are fragmented”**

### **⚠️ This sounds unnatural in spoken English**

People don’t usually say this directly.

### **What you *mean*:**

The explanation is:

* unclear  
* scattered  
* not structured

### **✅ Better ways to say it:**

* “I think the explanation is a bit fragmented.”  
* “Can you walk me through it more clearly?”  
* “I’m not fully following — could you structure it a bit?”

👉 Softer and more professional tone.

* “I feel we’re jumping between points a bit.”  
* “Could you walk through it step by step?”  
* “Sorry, I’m not fully following — can you clarify?”

## **4\. “Narrowed it down”**

You reduced possibilities and **identified the likely cause**.

👉 Very common in debugging discussions.

* “We’ve narrowed it down to the RAE trigger logic.”

* “We’ve narrowed it down to two possible issues.”  
* “After checking the logs, we narrowed it down further.”  
* “So far, it looks like the problem is in this module.”

## **🔥 Natural Meeting Flow (Combine All)**

Practice saying this:

“It looks like this never made its way to production.  
 Where are you with the investigation so far?  
 I’m not fully following — could you walk me through it step by step?  
 That being said, have we narrowed it down to a specific issue?”

## **🎯 Key Speaking Tip (Important for You)**

Your thoughts are correct, but:  
 👉 Sometimes you sound **too direct or incomplete**

To improve:

* Add softeners: *“I think,” “maybe,” “could you…”*  
* Add connectors: *“that being said,” “so far,” “for example”*

## **💪 Practice Task**

Say this aloud:

“We’ve narrowed it down, but I want to confirm one thing — did this ever make it to production?”

# 🗣 Spoken English Practice (Use in Meetings)

Here are some natural sentences you can practice aloud:

1. “At first everything looked fine, but over time, some issues started creeping in.”  
2. “If we’re not careful, scope creep can creep in during the later phases.”  
3. “Initially the model was stable; however, bias started creeping in after retraining.”  
4. “That being said, small errors can creep in when we scale the pipeline.”  
5. “On the other hand, if we automate validation, these problems won’t creep in.”

Practice saying these slowly and clearly. Focus on natural pauses.
